## Assistant <sup>9/4/2026, 11:53:33 AM</sup>

Press **Shift+Tab** in the chat input — it cycles the permission mode: normal → auto-accept edits → plan mode → back to normal. The current mode is shown just below the input box, so you'll see `plan mode on` once you land on it.

Alternatives:
- Click the mode indicator/dropdown below the input box and pick **Plan** directly.
- Type `/plan` if you prefer a command.

To leave plan mode, either keep pressing Shift+Tab, or approve the plan when I present it — approval exits plan mode automatically.

I'd like to build an entity olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin which approximates one quarter of a sign wave (one quadrant).

I want to have this a sone entity with one model. The output format of the sine-wave shall be an argument/generic. The format always must be in the form (1, 0/1, N). If there aere 0 integer bits, the sine-wave shall be scaled to 1.0-1LSB. IF the is 1 integer bit, the sine-wave is unscaled (peak at 1.0),

I want it for 10-20 fractional bits. For every resolution there shall be an optimized table (with exactly as much memory size as needeD) - for both S=1 and S=0.

I also want the option to generate sin/cos output from the same table (with two read ports).
How do we best realize this? 

I think it is best to generate the olo_fix_lin_qpprox_qsin by the python code generator and gen manually edit the HDL as needed. Do you agree?

I'd suggest to provide a olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin python class which contains all table configurations, which wrapps the olo_fix_lin_approx. This way the user has an exact python/vhdl correspondence. do you agree?

Now to the big question: Do we generate the tables in VHDL procedurally or use generated tables from python? I feel like using the generated tables has less risk for mismatches due to rounding differences in sin() / cos() between VHDL simulator and python. Do you agree?

I am OK with figuring out the best settings (offset/gradient resolution) for every output format myself. But I'd like you to provide an efficient flow and template to copy then into - and if needed to copy the tables in VHDL into.

## Assistant <sup>9/4/2026, 12:11:09 PM</sup>

I've read the full `olo_fix_lin_approx` stack (model, template, `olo_fix_lin_approx_calc`, codegen hookup, docs). Short answers to your four questions first:

**1. Generate with the Python generator, then hand-edit — partly.** The existing generator emits *one entity per fixed configuration*. What you want is *one entity with 22 tables selected by a generic*, which the template cannot express. So: use `generate_entity()` once to get a reference file, but the deliverable should be a **hand-written `olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.vhd`** plus a **machine-generated table package** that is regenerated whenever a table config changes. Hand-editing generated table data would be a maintenance trap.

**2. A `olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin` Python class wrapping `olo_fix_lin_approx` — fully agree.** It holds the config table (one `olo_fix_lin_approx_cfg` per resolution), picks the entry from `out_fmt`, and delegates `process()`. That gives exact Python/VHDL correspondence for free, and the cosim reuses the existing VC flow.

**3. Generated tables, not procedural VHDL — agree, strongly.** `ieee.math_real.sin` accuracy is implementation-defined; Questa, GHDL, Vivado and NVC will not agree at the LSB, and `cl_fix_from_real` rounding at a tie amplifies that into a table-entry mismatch. Baked-in constants are deterministic across every tool and bit-true against Python by construction. The cost (a large generated file) is real but contained if the tables live in their own package.

**4. Structure** — `olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.vhd` instantiates `olo_fix_lin_approx_calc` once (sin) or twice (sin+cos), both reading one ROM through two read ports. The cos instance simply gets the **bit-complement of the phase input**: `~p` mirrors both the table index and the remainder, so no change to `olo_fix_lin_approx_calc` is needed.

That last point has a consequence worth your decision, so let me ask before writing the plan.

## Assistant <sup>9/4/2026, 12:18:52 PM</sup>

Confirmed — `olo_base_ram_tdp.vhd:347,362` reads one `shared variable` from two processes, which is exactly the dual-port ROM idiom I need.

One important interaction between your answers: **half-LSB convention + free `InFmt_g` would normally force the table to depend on the phase width** (the offset δ = ½·LSB_phase gets baked into the table). There's a way to get both — apply the half-LSB in the *datapath* instead of the table by appending a constant `'1'` bit below the phase LSB. Then `x_sin = p & '1'` and `x_cos = (not p) & '1'`, and `x_sin + x_cos = 1.0` **exactly**, for any phase width, with a plain M-independent table. I've built the plan on that.

```json
  "content": "# olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin - Quarter Sine Approximation\n\n## Context\n\n`olo_fix_lin_approx` (Python model + code generator) and `olo_fix_lin_approx_calc` (VHDL datapath) exist on\n`feature/lin-approx`. ## Assistant <sup>9/4/2026, 12:21:31 PM</sup>
```

Good call — and the folding logic makes the case clearer than I'd have guessed. **Merge them into one entity.** Reasoning:

Split the M-bit rotation phase into `[q1 q0 | p]`. Sin needs the *mirrored* quarter address `~p` whenever `q0 = 1`, so a sin-only user already needs the mirroring — a quarter-only entity would not save them anything. And the cos address turns out to be exactly the complement of the sin address (`q0' = not q0` after adding a quadrant), so your two-read-port idea drops straight out:

```
a_sin = p xor (q0 replicated)       -- quarter address, M-2 bits
x_sin = a_sin       & '1'           -- half-LSB trick, (0,0,M-1)
x_cos = (not a_sin) & '1'
negate sin if q1;  negate cos if (q1 xor q0)
```

I verified the half-LSB convention survives the folding in all four quadrants: the effective phase is `theta + 0.5*LSB_theta` for **both** channels, identically, and `x_sin + x_cos = 1.0` exactly. A separate `qsin` entity would be ~20 lines of reuse against a full doc/TB/registration burden, so it isn't worth it. The quarter-sine tables stay quarter-sine — that's just the package content now.

Rewriting the plan.

Code generation: The entity si not "hand-written" but "written by claude-code spearately".

InFmt_g shall be anything. If it is a range of less than 1.0, then only a part of the sine wave is approximated (and some efficiency loss on the table size accepted). IF itis > 1.0, it wrapps (higher bits are simply omitted).

## Assistant <sup>9/4/2026, 12:33:52 PM</sup>

## Assistant <sup>9/4/2026, 12:33:59 PM</sup>

Good — the critical-angle idea is better than I gave it credit for. Direct answer first:

**On its own it's additive, not simpler** — it only covers 4 of the 2^M input codes, so you still need the folding for everything else. **But combined with a two's-complement mirror it replaces the half-LSB trick entirely**, which is a net win:

The reason I needed the half-LSB offset was that one's-complement mirroring (`~p`) lands one LSB short of `1-p`. Two's complement (`-p`) is *exact* — except at `p = 0`, where it wraps to 0 and the table returns ≈0 instead of ±1. And `p = 0` is exactly your four critical angles. So:

```
n     = -p                        -- two's complement, one (M-2)-bit negate
a_sin = q0 ? n : p                -- exact 1-p mirror
a_cos = q0 ? p : n
p = 0  ->  force {sin,cos} from the constant table per quadrant
```

Result: exact phase (no half-LSB caveat), `sin(0)=0`, `cos(0)=1`, peaks exactly ±1, and the calc input is a bit *narrower* than the half-LSB version. Cost is one small negate, an all-zeros detect and a constant mux — roughly a wash against the reasoning it removes. Recommending it.

On `InFmt_g` being anything: that falls out of one resize. `Phase = cl_fix_resize(In_Data, InFmt_g, (0,0,F), Trunc_s, None_s)` drops integer bits (wrap), wraps the sign (so `-0.3` reads as `0.7`, correct since sine is periodic in rotations), and zero-pads when `I < 0` so only part of the wave is reachable — full table still built, as you accepted.

## Assistant <sup>9/4/2026, 12:45:52 PM</sup>

Understood — that's a better pattern, and it generalizes cleanly. Let me restate the split:

- **`olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin`** — the primitive: quarter phase `a` in `[0,1)` → `sin(pi/2*a)`, optional `cos(pi/2*a)` via the mirrored address `1-a` on a second read port. Owns the tables and the `a=0` correction (`cos(0)=1`, whose mirror wraps).
- **`olo_fix_sin`** — range reduction: wrap the input to one rotation, split off the 2 quadrant bits, feed `p` to `qsin`, then map.

There's one nice consequence of feeding `p` directly rather than a folded address: since `sin(pi/2*(1-a)) = cos(pi/2*a)`, mirroring is the same as **swapping** the two qsin outputs. So the quadrant map is pure muxing, with no address arithmetic and no critical-angle table in `olo_fix_sin`:

| q | Out_Sin | Out_Cos |
|---|---------|---------|
| 0 | +qs.sin | +qs.cos |
| 1 | +qs.cos | −qs.sin |
| 2 | −qs.sin | −qs.cos |
| 3 | −qs.cos | +qs.sin |

At `p=0` this gives exactly `(0,±peak)`/`(±peak,0)` for free. One trade-off falls out of it though, and it's yours to make:


---

```json
{
  "questions": [
    {
      "question": "With the swap-based quadrant map, olo_fix_sin needs BOTH qsin outputs even when only sin is requested (in odd quadrants, sin comes from the qsin cos channel). A sin-only olo_fix_sin therefore costs two table reads and two multipliers. How should olo_fix_sin do the folding?",
      "header": "Folding",
      "multiSelect": false,
      "options": [
        {
          "label": "Swap only (Recommended)",
          "description": "olo_fix_sin is pure muxing: no address arithmetic, no critical-angle handling, all special-casing lives in qsin's a=0 path. Always two qsin lookups, so CosOutput_g on olo_fix_sin saves only the output mux, not a DSP. Simplest and easiest to prove correct."
        },
        {
          "label": "Mirror address when cos is off",
          "description": "Classic single-read quarter-wave DDS: when CosOutput_g=false, olo_fix_sin feeds a = q0 ? -p : p and uses one qsin channel, saving one ROM port and one multiplier. Costs a conditional-negate adder plus a critical-angle constant mux in olo_fix_sin (needed because a=1.0 is not representable at p=0), i.e. correction logic duplicated with qsin. Two code paths in the entity."
        },
        {
          "label": "Mirror address always",
          "description": "olo_fix_sin always folds the address and uses qsin's sin channel for sin and cos channel for cos. Uniform single code path, saves the second lookup when cos is off, but always pays the conditional-negate adder and always needs the critical-angle constants in olo_fix_sin."
        }
      ]
    }
  ]
}
```

The approximation itself is done by\n-- olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin, which covers one quadrant. The two MSBs of the phase select the quadrant,\n-- the remaining bits are the in-quadrant phase. In the odd quadrants the quarter phase is mirrored\n-- and depending on the quadrant the result is negated.\n--\n-- Any input format is allowed. Integer bits are dropped (the phase wraps, because the sine is\n-- periodic with a period of one rotation) and negative values wrap as well. Formats covering less\n-- than one rotation only reach a part of the wave.\n--\n-- Documentation:\n-- https://github.com/open-logic/open-logic/blob/main/doc/fix/olo_fix_sin.md\n--\n-- Note: The link points to the documentation of the latest release. Otherwise a sweep, the codes at the critical\n    angles (where the mirrored address wraps) and their neighbours are used.\n    \"\"\"\n    width = cl_fix_width(fmt)\n    all_codes = 2**width\n\n    if all_codes <= samples:\n        codes = np.arange(all_codes)\n    else:\n        sweep = np.linspace(0, all_codes - 1, samples).astype(np.int64)\n        step = min(max(critical_step, 1), all_codes)\n        critical = np.arange(0, all_codes, step)\n        # The neighbours of the critical codes are the worst case for the approximation\n        codes = np.concatenate((sweep, critical, critical + 1, critical - 1))\n        codes = np.unique(np.mod(codes, all_codes))\n\n    # Convert to the signed integer representation expected by cl_fix_from_integer\n    if fmt.S == 1:\n        codes = np.where(codes >= 2**(width - 1), codes - 2**width, codes)\n

```
import os
import importlib

# Get the current directory
current_dir = os.path.dirname(__file__)

# Recursively import all Python files in subdirectories
for root, dirs, files in os.walk(current_dir):
    for file in files:
        if file.endswith(".py") and file != "__init__.py":
            # Construct the module name relative to the package
            relative_path = os.path.relpath(os.path.join(root, file), current_dir)
            module_name = relative_path.replace(os.sep, ".")[:-3]  # Remove ".py" extension

            try:
                # Import the module dynamically
                importlib.import_module(f".{module_name}", package=__name__)
            except ImportError as e:
                print(f"Failed to import {module_name}: {e}")test/fix/__init__.py
```

```
end function;

    function fixImplementReg (
        logicPresent : boolean;
        regMode      : string) return boolean is
        variable Result_v : boolean := false;
    begin

        -- Calculate register requirement
        if compareNoCase(regMode, "yes") then
            Result_v := true;
        elsif compareNoCase(regMode, "no") then
            Result_v := false;
        elsif compareNoCase(regMode, "auto") then
            Result_v := logicPresent;

            -- synthesis translate_on
            -- coverage on
        end if;
```

## Assistant <sup>9/4/2026, 1:00:29 PM</sup>

`fixImplementReg(..., "YES")` returns true unconditionally, and `olo_fix_lin_approx_calc` hardcodes `RoundReg_g/SatReg_g => "YES"` — so its latency is a constant 8 regardless of round/saturate. My pipe depth is correct. Writing the testbenches with explicit latency checks to catch any future drift.

Compared to\n[olo_fix_cordic_rot](./olo_fix_cordic_rot.md) it trades memory for latency and logic: it needs a ROM but only\n9 to 10 clock cycles instead of one iteration per output bit.\n\n_olo_fix_sin_ implements the **range reduction** only. The approximation itself is done by\n[olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin](./olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.md), which covers one quadrant.\n\n**Latency** of this entity is 10 clock cycles. The entity is fully pipelined, hence it accepts one input sample per\nclock cycle. As a result, back-pressure is not supported.\n\nFor details about the fixed-point number format used in _Open Logic_, refer to the\n[fixed point principles](./olo_fix_principles.md).\n\n### Output Format and Scaling\n\n_OutFmt_g_ must be `(1, 0, N)` or `(1, 1, N)` with _N_

## Assistant <sup>9/4/2026, 1:17:13 PM</sup>

Confirmed a real inconsistency: with `CosOutput_g=false`, `Out_Cos` emits `Peak_c` on zero-phase samples instead of the documented zeros. Fixing both that and the elaboration-guard issue.


---

## Assistant <sup>9/4/2026, 1:17:21 PM</sup>

<details>
<summary>Tool: <code>Bash</code></summary>

```json
{
  "command": "python3 - <<'EOF'\n# --- Fix 1: qsin Out_Cos must be all zeros when CosOutput_g is false ---\np = \"src/fix/vhdl/olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.vhd\"\ns = open(p).read()\nold = \"\"\"    -- *** Outputs ***\n    Out_Valid <= r.Out_Valid;\n    Out_Sin   <= r.Out_Sin;\n    Out_Cos   <= r.Out_Cos;\n\"\"\"\nnew = \"\"\"    -- *** Outputs ***\n    Out_Valid <= r.Out_Valid;\n    Out_Sin   <= r.Out_Sin;\n\n    g_cos_out : if CosOutput_g generate\n        Out_Cos <= r.Out_Cos;\n    end generate;\n\n    g_ncos_out : if not CosOutput_g generate\n        Out_Cos <= (others => '0');\n    end generate;\n\"\"\"\nassert old in s\ns = s.replace(old, new, 1)\nopen(p, \"w\").write(s)\n\n# --- Fix 2: olo_fix_sin - keep elaboration alive for InFmt_g.F < 3 so the assertion is reachable ---\np = \"src/fix/vhdl/olo_fix_sin.vhd\"\ns = open(p).read()\nold = \"\"\"    -- Phase word - the phase wraps into one rotation, hence integer bits and the sign are dropped\n    constant PhaseFmt_c  : FixFormat_t := (0, 0, InFmt_c.F);\n    constant PhaseBits_c : positive    := InFmt_c.F;\"\"\"\nnew = \"\"\"    -- Phase word - the phase wraps into one rotation, hence integer bits and the sign are dropped.\n    -- At least three bits are required (two quadrant bits plus one bit of in-quadrant phase). The\n    -- maximum keeps elaboration alive for illegal formats so the assertion below can report them.\n    constant PhaseBits_c : positive    := max(InFmt_c.F, 3);\n    constant PhaseFmt_c  : FixFormat_t := (0, 0, PhaseBits_c);\"\"\"\nassert old in s\ns = s.replace(old, new, 1)\nopen(p, \"w\").write(s)\nprint(\"both fixes applied\")\nEOF\ncd lint/script && vsg -c ../config/vsg_config.yml -f ../../src/fix/vhdl/olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.vhd ../../src/fix/vhdl/olo_fix_sin.vhd --all_phases -of summary 2>&1 | tail -4"
}
```

```
package olo_fix_private_lin_approx_qsin_pkg is

    -- Width one table entry is padded to. The entries of all tables share the same width so they
    -- can be stored in one array type. The padding is meant to be sliced away by the entity, hence
    -- it does not cost any memory.
    constant LinApproxEntryWidth_c : positive := 36;

    -- Types
    subtype LinApproxEntry_t is std_logic_vector(LinApproxEntryWidth_c - 1 downto 0);
    type LinApproxTable_t is array (natural range <>) of LinApproxEntry_t;

    -- Table content of the approximation with the given name. Each entry contains the gradient
    -- (MSBs) and the offset (LSBs), zero padded to LinApproxEntryWidth_c. The number of points is
    -- available through the 'length attribute of the value returned.
    function getTable (Name : string) return LinApproxTable_t;
```

```
def _reference_model(out_fmt : FixFormat):
        """
        Model used to derive the table content of an output format

        The table content does not depend on the phase resolution, hence the recommended resolution
        of the configuration is used.
        """
        return olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin(out_fmt, FixFormat(0, 0, qsin_min_quarter_bits(out_fmt)))

    @staticmethod
    def _fmt_string(fmt : FixFormat) -> str:
        """
        String representation of a FixFormat as expected by olo_fix string generics
        """
        return f"({fmt.S}, {fmt.I}, {fmt.F})"

    @staticmethod
    def _hex_string(grad, grad_fmt : FixFormat, offs, offs_fmt : FixFormat, width : int) -> str:
```

## Assistant <sup>9/6/2026, 11:33:14 AM</sup>

Refactor done and fully verified.

**What moved**

`olo_fix_lin_approx.generate_package(approximations, package_name, directory)` is now a static method in [olo_fix_lin_approx.py:279](src/fix/python/olo_fix/olo_fix_lin_approx.py#L279), driven by the reusable template [olo_fix_lin_approx_pkg_vhdl.template](src/fix/python/olo_fix/templates/olo_fix_lin_approx_pkg_vhdl.template). It takes `dict[str, olo_fix_lin_approx]` and emits exactly the interface you specified:

```vhdl
function getTable       (Name : string) return LinApproxTable_t;
function getOffsetFmt   (Name : string) return string;
function getGradientFmt (Name : string) return string;
```

Point count comes from `getTable(Name)'length`, so no fourth getter was needed. Unknown names fail with a message that lists the available tables.

`olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.generate_package(directory)` is now a 10-line assembler that builds the dict and delegates — no custom codegen left. Any future `olo_fix_lin_approx_inv_18b` / `_sqrt` uses the same call.

**Follow-on cleanups this enabled**
- Dropped the drift-guard unit test and its `tempfile` machinery — as you said, the co-simulation covers it (HDL uses the checked-in package, Python uses `QSIN_TABLES`; drift fails the cosim). `generate_package` is `# pragma: no cover`.
- `min_quarter_bits` left the per-table config — it was always `F+2`. It's now one rule (`MIN_QUARTER_BITS_MARGIN`) mirrored by `MinInBits_c` in the HDL, so `QSIN_TABLES` is down to the three values you actually tune.
- The qsin-specific package functions (`qsinPoints`/`qsinOffsFmt`/`qsinGradFmt`/`qsinPeak`/`qsinIsSupported`/`qsinMinQuarterBits`) are gone. Peak is now a 5-line local `peakValue` function in each of the two entities.

**Verification**
- Full `*olo_fix*` regression: all passed (includes the pre-existing `olo_fix_lin_approx_*` generated TBs, so `generate_entity`/`generate_tb` are unaffected by the `_render` change to a staticmethod).
- 223 Python tests pass; **100 %** statement coverage on `olo_fix_lin_approx.py`, `olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.py` and `olo_fix_sin.py`.
- VSG clean on all tracked `src/**` and `test/fix/**` VHDL, and on the generated package with the overlay.
- Regenerating the package is byte-identical to the checked-in file.

One judgement call worth flagging: the package's type and function names (`LinApproxTable_t`, `getTable`, …) are deliberately generic, so two such packages can't both be `use`d unqualified in one architecture without ambiguity. That's fine for the one-table-package-per-entity pattern, but if you ever want an entity drawing on two families, it'll need selected names (`work.pkg_a.getTable(...)`).

```
# -*- coding: utf-8 -*-

from vsg import parser, violation
from vsg.rule_group import length


class file_length(length.Rule):
    """
    Checks the length of the file.

    Parameters
    ----------

    name : string
       The group the rule belongs to.

    identifier : string
       unique identifier.  Usually in the form of 00N.

    """

    def __init__(self):
        super().__init__()
        self.length = 2000

    def _get_tokens_of_interest(self, oFile):
        return oFile.get_tokens_matching([parser.carriage_return])

    def _analyze(self, lToi):
        if len(lToi) > self.length:
            sSolution = "Reduce file length to less than " + str(self.length) + " lines"
            oViolation = violation.New(self.length, None, sSolution)
            self.add_violation(oViolation)
```

## Assistant <sup>9/6/2026, 11:42:27 AM</sup>

Both changes are in and everything is green.

**`getTableSize(<name>)` added** — your concern was justified for a different reason than expected: `olo_fix_sin` only needed the point count, but `getTable(...)'length` forced it to materialise a whole 1024-entry table constant at elaboration just to read an attribute off it. The new getter is a plain `IntegerArray_t` lookup, so both entities now use `getTableSize(TableName_c)` and no `'length` on a function return survives anywhere.

The package interface is now:

```vhdl
function getTable       (Name : string) return LinApproxTable_t;
function getTableSize   (Name : string) return positive;
function getOffsetFmt   (Name : string) return string;
function getGradientFmt (Name : string) return string;
```

**On the pragma — it isn't possible, so I took your second instruction.** `length_002` is implemented as `violation.New(self.length, None, sSolution)`; the `None` is where the line number goes, and VSG's pragma filter matches violations *by line*. With no line there is nothing for `-- vsg_off length_002` to match. I verified empirically too: not even a bare `-- vsg_off` at line 1 (disable *all* rules) suppresses it. So `length_002` cannot be disabled from source in VSG 3.27.0, in the file or the template.

Disabling it in [vsg_config.yml:2661](lint/config/vsg_config.yml#L2661) is the better outcome anyway — I reverted my `lint/script/script.py` change entirely and deleted the overlay file, so there is now **no** special-casing of generated files at all, and the generated package is linted against all 738 remaining rules (clean). One note: `lint/config/vsg_config_overlay_vc.yml` still carries its own `length_002: disable: true`, now redundant — I left it alone as an unrelated file, but it can go whenever you touch that file next.

**Verification after the changes:** full `*olo_fix*` regression all passed; 223 Python tests pass with 100 % statement coverage on all three modules; VSG clean across every tracked `src/**` and `test/fix/**` VHDL file including the generated package; NVC analyses the whole compile order without new warnings.

## Assistant <sup>9/6/2026, 11:57:23 AM</sup>

Understood — holding off on VHDL edits, simulations and linting from here.

Timing note: the change was already complete and verified when your message arrived, so the tree is currently self-consistent rather than half-done. Nothing is left dangling.

**Python model — what changed**

`in_fmt` is now a phase in **rotations** covering one quadrant, `(0, -2, N)`, with the check you asked for:

```python
if in_fmt.S != 0 or in_fmt.I != -2:
    raise ValueError(f"olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin: in_fmt {in_fmt} must be (0, -2, N) - the "
                     f"quarter phase covers one quadrant only, i.e. the range [0, 0.25) "
                     f"in rotations")
```

The index-resolution check moved from `in_fmt.F` to `cl_fix_width(in_fmt)`, since with `I = -2` the width is `N-2`.

Two consequences you'll care about while editing:

1. **`QSIN_TABLES` gradient formats changed** from `(0, 1, g)` to `(0, 3, g-2)`. The derivative is now taken over rotations, so it is 4× larger (max `2*pi ≈ 6.28`, needing 3 integer bits) while the remainder is 4× smaller. Same total width, and the stored integers are unchanged — regenerating the package produced a **46-line diff touching only the 22 `GradFmts_c` entries**, with all ~8000 table hex lines byte-identical. Good confirmation this is a pure reinterpretation.

2. **New helper `qsin_quarter_fmt(bits)`** returns `FixFormat(0, -2, bits + 2)`. `qsin_min_quarter_bits()` still means *significant* bits (`out_fmt.F + 2`), so the recommended format is `(0, -2, F+4)` — which is exactly `olo_fix_sin`'s recommended phase resolution, since the quarter phase is now just the in-quadrant slice of the rotation phase with no rescaling.

`olo_fix_sin` correspondingly passes `qsin_quarter_fmt(self._quarter_bits)` straight down.

**State when I stopped:** 223 Python tests pass, 100 % statement coverage on all three modules, report output sane (`GradMax` now reads ~6.28 as expected), and the last full VHDL regression + lint pass was green.

Since you're now iterating on the Python side: if you change `QSIN_TABLES` or the formats, the checked-in package will need `generate_package('../vhdl')` and the entities may need matching edits. Tell me when you want me to pick that back up.

## Assistant <sup>9/6/2026, 12:07:07 PM</sup>

Reviewed. The core idea is **right and genuinely simpler** — `0.25 - phase` is exactly the two's complement the HDL computes, and it is exactly representable for every non-zero phase, so no rounding sneaks in. I verified outputs are **identical** to the previous integer-address version across all exactly-representable inputs, so bit-trueness with the HDL is preserved.

Four defects, all on the *non-exactly-representable* input path (which is why the cosim can't catch them — its stimuli come from `cl_fix_from_integer`):

**1. List input now raises `TypeError`** — `0.25 - phase` on a `list`. Lists are an accepted input elsewhere in olo_fix (`test_olo_fix_abs` passes one). Previously worked, since the arithmetic happened after `cl_fix_from_real`.

**2. The zero override tests the raw input instead of the quantized one.** `phase == 0.0` should be `sin_phase == 0.0`. Anything that quantizes to zero without being literally `0.0` skips the override, while the HDL applies it — it only ever sees the quantized word. Worse, for a list input `phase == 0.0` evaluates to the scalar `False`, silently disabling the override for the whole batch.

**3. sin and cos can be evaluated one LSB apart.** Because cos is mirrored from the *raw* input, the invariant `0.25 - cos_phase == sin_phase` — which the HDL satisfies by construction, having a single input word — breaks for **101 of 269** half-LSB test inputs. Near 90° that costs up to 0.28 LSB of extra cos error (0.893 vs 0.612 LSB).

**4. Spurious saturation warning at phase 0.** `0.25 - 0 = 0.25` exceeds the format maximum, so every batch containing zero emits `NarrowFix: Number 0.25 exceeds maximum for format (0, -2, 20)`. Zero is a critical angle and is in the cosim stimuli, so this fires routinely.

All four are fixed by deriving the mirror from the quantized phase:

```python
        sin_phase = cl_fix_from_real(phase, self.in_fmt)
        # The mirrored phase is exact for every phase /= 0. For phase = 0 it exceeds the range, but
        # that case is corrected below anyway - hence saturation without a warning.
        cos_phase = cl_fix_from_real(0.25 - sin_phase, self.in_fmt, FixSaturate.Sat_s)

        # Approximation
        sin_val = self._approx.process(sin_phase)
        cos_val = self._approx.process(cos_phase)

        # A phase of zero is exact and its mirrored phase wraps, hence it is handled separately
        sin_val = np.where(sin_phase == 0.0, 0.0, sin_val)
        cos_val = np.where(sin_phase == 0.0, self.peak, cos_val)
```

Verified against the current version: identical on representable inputs, invariant holds 269/269, no warnings, lists work.

Two cosmetic points: the class docstring at [olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.py:139-142](src/fix/python/olo_fix/olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.py#L139-L142) now ends `...0 to 90 degrees:` with nothing after the colon (the formula block and the mirroring explanation went with the edit), and `next(phase)` vs `process(quarter_phase)` disagree on the parameter name.

I haven't touched the file since you're editing it — say the word and I'll apply the patch.

```
def next(self, phase):
        """
        Process next N samples

        :param phase: Phase in rotations, in the range [0, 0.25), i.e. 0 to 90 degrees
        :return: Tuple (sine, cosine)
        """
        # Convert scalars to 1d array and quantize
        if np.isscalar(phase):
            phase = np.array([phase])
        sin_phase = cl_fix_from_real(phase, self.in_fmt)
        cos_phase = cl_fix_from_real(0.25 - phase, self.in_fmt, FixSaturate.None_s)

        # Approximation
        sin_val = self._approx.process(sin_phase)
        cos_val = self._approx.process(cos_phase)

        # An input of zero is exact and its mirrored address wraps, hence it is handled separately
        sin_val = np.where(phase == 0.0, 0.0, sin_val)
        cos_val = np.where(phase == 0.0, self.peak, cos_val)

        return sin_val, cos_val
```

## Assistant <sup>9/6/2026, 12:19:26 PM</sup>

The CLI is in and working, but the more important thing I hit along the way:

## `FixSaturate.None_s` broke the model

Your edit to line 226 made `next()` raise on **every** call — 14 of 20 tests were failing. `NarrowFix.from_real` never implemented wrapping:

```python
if (saturate == FixSaturate.Sat_s) or (saturate == FixSaturate.SatWarn_s):
    ...saturate...
else:
    raise NotImplementedError(f"NarrowFix: Unsupported saturation mode {str(saturate)}")
```

It raises unconditionally for `None_s`, not just when the value is out of range. I switched it to `Sat_s`, which suppresses the spurious warning just as well (only `SatWarn_s`/`Warn_s` warn) and gives an identical result, since the sole out-of-range case is `phase = 0` and that one is overridden anyway. 223 tests pass again, 100 % coverage on all three modules.

Worth noting your instinct was right in principle — `None_s` (wrap) is what the HDL does, and wrapping `0.25` gives `0.0`, exactly the two's complement of zero. It just isn't available in this Python model.

## The CLI

Added `main()` at the end of the module, plus `if __name__ == "__main__"`. Both are `# pragma: no cover`.

```
python3 -m olo_fix.olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin --generate
python3 -m olo_fix.olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin --analyze "(1,0,15)"
```

- `--generate` writes to `<root>/src/fix/vhdl`, derived from `__file__` so the working directory doesn't matter. Verified byte-identical to the previously generated package.
- `--analyze` builds the reference model at the recommended resolution and calls `analyze()`. Since you removed `analyze()` from the class, it goes through `model._approx.analyze()` — which prints the gradient/offset ranges, table width and max error in LSB, then plots.
- Bad input gets a proper `parser.error()` instead of a traceback:
  ```
  error: --analyze: 'nonsense' is not a number format - use the form "(1,0,15)"
  error: --analyze: olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin: out_fmt (1, 0, 9) is not supported (supported: (1, 0/1, 10..20))
  ```
- No action prints the help.

Two things to be aware of. It has to run via `-m` because the module uses a relative import; a direct `python3 olo_fix/olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.py` fails. And `-m` emits a benign `RuntimeWarning` from runpy, because `olo_fix/__init__.py` already imported the module — harmless here, but I couldn't remove it without contorting the package.

Also still outstanding from the earlier review, untouched: the zero override tests the raw `phase` rather than the quantized `sin_phase` (so it misses inputs that quantize to zero, and silently no-ops on list input), and `0.25 - phase` mirrors the raw input, which lets sin and cos land one LSB apart.

## Assistant <sup>9/6/2026, 12:26:33 PM</sup>

Two separate problems — and the one blocking you isn't the invocation.

**The command is:**

```bash
cd src/fix/python
python3 -m olo_fix.olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin --generate
python3 -m olo_fix.olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin --analyze "(1,0,15)"
```

Running the file directly (`python3 olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.py`) can never work: line 9 is `from en_cl_fix_pkg import *`, and the path to `en_cl_fix` is set up in `olo_fix/__init__.py`, which a direct run bypasses. You get `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'en_cl_fix_pkg'` — that's probably what you saw.

**But right now even `-m` fails**, before `main()` is ever reached:

```
ImportError: cannot import name 'qsin_quarter_fmt' from 'olo_fix.olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin'
```

Your last edits removed `MIN_QUARTER_BITS_MARGIN`, `qsin_min_quarter_bits()` and `qsin_quarter_fmt()`, but there are still 19 references to them, which makes the whole `olo_fix` package unimportable:

- [olo_fix_sin.py:13](src/fix/python/olo_fix/olo_fix_sin.py#L13) and [olo_fix_sin.py:77](src/fix/python/olo_fix/olo_fix_sin.py#L77)
- 17 references in [test_olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.py](src/fix/python/tests/test_olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.py)

`_reference_model` already inlines the format as `FixFormat(0, -2, out_fmt.F + 2)`, so the mechanical fix is to do the same in `olo_fix_sin` (`FixFormat(0, -2, self._quarter_bits + 2)`) and update the tests. Want me to apply that?

One thing to be aware of in that inlined version: it is `(0, -2, F+2)`, i.e. **width F**, whereas the old `qsin_quarter_fmt(qsin_min_quarter_bits(...))` gave `(0, -2, F+4)`, width F+2. So the reference model now runs two bits below the resolution the accuracy budget was designed around. The generated package is unaffected — table centers depend only on `in_fmt.I` and `points`, not on the fractional bits — but `--analyze` will report a correspondingly larger error. Intentional?

If the `-m` form annoys you (it also emits a harmless `RuntimeWarning` from runpy, because `__init__.py` has already imported the module), I can add a small `src/fix/python/qsin.py` wrapper that does the `sys.path` setup and calls `main()`, matching how `cosim.py` and `lin_approx_codegen.py` are already run in this repo. Then it'd just be `python3 qsin.py --generate`.

```
        qsin_val, qcos_val = self._qsin.process(qphase)

        sin_val = np.where(qphase == 0.0, 0.0, qsin_val)
        cos_val = np.where(qphase == 0.0, self.peak, qcos_val)

        # Quadrant mapping - the quarter wave is mirrored and negated depending on the quadrant.
        # The negation is exact, because the negated value is representable in the output format.
        sin_val_out = np.choose(quadrant, [sin_val,  cos_val, -sin_val, -cos_val])
        cos_val_out = np.choose(quadrant, [cos_val, -sin_val, -cos_val,  sin_val])

        return sin_val_out, cos_val_out
```

</details>

## Assistant <sup>9/6/2026, 1:00:14 PM</sup>

Vectorized:

```python
# Quadrant mapping - the quarter wave is mirrored and negated depending on the quadrant.
# The negation is exact, because the negated value is representable in the output format.
sin_val_out = np.choose(quadrant, [sin_val,  cos_val, -sin_val, -cos_val])
cos_val_out = np.choose(quadrant, [cos_val, -sin_val, -cos_val,  sin_val])
```

The old form couldn't work on arrays at all — `if quadrant == 0` on an ndarray raises *"truth value of an array is ambiguous"*, and it also had no `else`, so `sin_val_out` could be unbound.

Verified: errors stay under 1 LSB across output/input format combinations, the critical angles come out exact, `sin(x+0.5) == -sin(x)` and `cos(x+0.25) == -sin(x)` hold **bit-exactly**, and scalar input, shape and dtype are preserved. One cosmetic note: negated zeros now surface as `-0.0` (e.g. `sin(0.5)`), which compares equal to `0.0` everywhere and converts to integer `0`, so it's harmless.

Three things I ran into that are outside what you asked, but you'll want to know:

**1. The zero-padding path is gone, and it's a regression.** Line 70 sets `self.qphase_fmt = FixFormat(0, -2, self.in_fmt.F)`, which ignores `self._quarter_bits` / `self._pad_bits` computed at lines 64-65 — those are now dead. So when `in_fmt.F - 2 <= index_bits` the qsin constructor rejects it:

```
ValueError: in_fmt (0, -2, 8) must be wider than 8 bits for a table with 256 points
```

That breaks `InFmt_g = (0,0,8)` and `(0,0,3)`, which are covered both by `test_accuracy_input_formats` and by two sim configs. The old code padded at the LSB end so any input format stayed legal.

**2. Dead / shadowed code in `next()`.** `quadrant` is computed twice — line 99 via `cl_fix_resize` to `quadrant_fmt`, then overwritten at line 107 by `phase_int >> (self._phase_bits - 2)`. Line 108's `in_quad` is unused (`qphase` from line 100 is used instead), as are `phase_int` and `_phase_bits` beyond that. Both quadrant computations agree, so it's cosmetic, but only one should survive — the `cl_fix_resize` version at line 99 reads better and matches how `qphase` is derived.

**3. The test file still doesn't import**, so I can't run the suite: `test_olo_fix_lin_approx_qsin.py` imports `qsin_min_quarter_bits` and `qsin_quarter_fmt`, which you removed. That's the same blocker as before — `olo_fix_sin.py` is now fixed, the test file isn't.

Want me to clean up 1-3?
